Used the more recent `New token.txt`, verified with a `git ls-remote` before doing anything else; it worked. Also present but untouched (not referenced by this task's instructions): `sz-auth-update.tar.gz` and the `repo` checkout, exactly as flagged by a prior session — left both alone again.

Picked backlog item #15 (character profiles) as the night's work. Item #3 (deployment decision) is an explicit human call, not a coding task; item #6c (email notifications) is blocked on SMTP credentials that don't exist; items #9-11 are open-ended "worth revisiting" notes rather than scoped, buildable features; #15 was the next item in the list with a clear spec and an explicit "feasible now" read, and unblocks #17 and #19 later.

Implementation: a new `characters` table (`id`, `user_id`, `name`, `archetype`, `bio`, `backstory`, `avatar_emoji`, timestamps) and `lib/characters.ts` (`createCharacter`/`updateCharacter`/`deleteCharacter`/`getCharacter`/`listCharactersForUser`, a `CharacterError` class, following the exact shape of `lib/profiles.ts`: trimming, per-field length caps — 100/150/1000/4000 chars for name/archetype/bio/backstory — and ownership checks on update/delete). `GET`/`POST /api/characters` and `PATCH`/`DELETE /api/characters/[id]` mirror the `profiles`/`sessionLog` route conventions (zod body validation, `requireUser()`, ownership errors mapped to 403). `/profile` gained a new `CharacterManager` client component (add/edit/delete, with a confirm step before delete, matching the existing leave-campaign confirm pattern) above the "My campaigns" section; the public `/players/[id]` page gained a read-only characters list. A new `CharacterSummary` presentational component is shared between the editable and read-only views so they can't drift apart.

Two judgment calls worth flagging:
* No image-upload infrastructure exists in this app, so "art/avatar placeholder" (as scoped in the backlog line) is a curated set of 20 fantasy-themed emoji rather than an uploaded picture — a real avatar-upload system is a much bigger, separate feature (storage, moderation, resizing) that wasn't in scope for a one-night item. A character's portrait defaults deterministically (a hash of the name into the emoji set) so it's never blank, and can be overridden from a dropdown.
* Kept "class/ancestry/role" as one free-text `archetype` field rather than per-system structured fields (a 5e class dropdown vs. a different system's archetype taxonomy, etc.) — this app is deliberately system-agnostic throughout (see `preferred_systems` and campaigns' own free-text `system` field), so a single free-text field matches that existing pattern rather than introducing a new per-system special case.
* Caught and fixed a real bug before it shipped: initially imported the new `CHARACTER_AVATARS` constant into the client component `CharacterManager.tsx` from `lib/characters.ts`, which imports `lib/db.ts` (better-sqlite3, a native Node module) — that would have pulled a DB driver into the browser bundle. `next build`'s successful compile doesn't catch this at the type level since it's still valid TypeScript; caught it by checking the existing convention (`lib/types.ts` already holds `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`/`NOTIFICATION_LABELS` as the client-safe home for shared constants, and `app/settings/notifications/page.tsx` never imports server `lib/` modules directly). Fixed by moving `CHARACTER_AVATARS` to `lib/types.ts` and having `lib/characters.ts` import and re-export it for server-side callers.

Testing: added `__tests__/unit/characters.test.ts` (10 tests) covering deterministic default-avatar assignment, an explicit avatar choice, blank-name rejection, all four per-field length limits, an invalid `avatarEmoji` value falling back to a valid default, per-user listing (newest-first, isolated from other users), partial updates, ownership enforcement on edit/delete, actual deletion, and not-found errors. 91 → 101 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Also ran an ad hoc manual smoke test against a real `next start` server on a spare port: unauthenticated `GET /api/characters` → 401; signup → empty list; create a character → 201 with the expected fields; an oversized bio → 400; update → persisted; the public `/players/[id]` page rendered the character's name and updated bio; delete → 200 and the list was empty again.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c, still blocked) or the deployment decision (#3, still a human call).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: merging two parallel backlog #15 implementations)
This session had already built and committed its own campaign-scoped character design locally (name, class/archetype, ancestry/lineage, backstory, a text/emoji avatar, scoped to one campaign per character) when `git push` was rejected as non-fast-forward: the automated nightly-build task had, unaware of this session, independently built and pushed its own account-scoped character design (`7d97d83`) in the same window. Fetched and read the other commit in full (`git show 7d97d83`) before doing anything else, rather than guessing at the conflict or force-pushing over it. The two designs were incompatible at the schema level (two different `characters` tables) and reflected genuinely different product interpretations of the same backlog line, not a simple merge-conflict-in-code situation — so this was surfaced to the owner as a real decision (keep one, keep the other, or combine both) rather than resolved unilaterally. The owner chose to merge both.

Design of the merge: reset this session's scratch clone to `origin/main` (the nightly build's now-canonical `7d97d83`), keeping its schema, `lib/characters.ts` conventions (account-scoped `createCharacter(userId, input)`/`updateCharacter(characterId, userId, input)`/`deleteCharacter`, the curated `CHARACTER_AVATARS` emoji set with deterministic-hash defaulting, and the `/profile`-based `CharacterManager` UI) as the base, since it was already the one pushed to `main`. Added a single nullable `campaign_id` column on top of it rather than reintroducing a second table or a second CRUD surface — a character still always belongs to its owner first (created/edited/deleted only via the account-level `/api/characters` routes, always visible on that owner's `/players/[id]`), and can *additionally* be linked to one campaign at a time. Linking (on create via `campaignId` in the POST body, or on update via `campaignId` in the PATCH body — `undefined` leaves the link alone, `null` unlinks, a campaign id (re)links) reuses `hasPrivateAccess(userId, campaignId)` — the exact same DM-or-approved-member check as party notes and session logs — so a character can't be attached to a campaign its owner isn't actually part of; unlinking never needs that check, since removing a claim can't spoof anything the way adding one could. `listCharactersForCampaign(campaignId)` is new, public (no access check, matching the roster's own visibility), and feeds a new read-only "Characters at this table" block on the campaign detail page (next to `RosterPanel`) via the existing `CharacterSummary` presentational component — so all three surfaces (`/profile`'s editable list, `/players/[id]`'s read-only list, and now the campaign page's read-only list) render from the same component and can't drift apart. `CharacterSummary` gained an optional `linkedCampaign` prop (shown everywhere except the campaign's own page, where it would be redundant) that links back to the campaign. `GET /api/characters` now enriches each character with its linked campaign's current title server-side, so the profile page can show/link to it without a second round trip and stays correct even for a campaign the owner has since left. `CharacterManager`'s create/edit form gained a "Playing in" `<select>` populated from the signed-in user's own current (non-cancelled) campaigns — passed down from `/profile`'s existing `dming`/`playing` state — with a fallback option injected if a character is linked to a campaign that's since fallen out of that list, so editing a character never silently drops an existing link just because the dropdown re-rendered.

Testing: kept all 10 of the nightly build's original `characters.test.ts` tests verbatim (still valid against the merged `lib/characters.ts`, which is backward-compatible with every call site that doesn't pass `campaignId`) and appended 9 new tests covering: creating a character pre-linked to a campaign the owner is DMing or an approved member of; rejecting a link to a campaign the owner has no access to, and to a campaign that doesn't exist; defaulting to unlinked; linking, relinking to a different campaign, and unlinking via update; rejecting a relink to an inaccessible campaign while confirming the existing link is left untouched; confirming an omitted `campaignId` on update leaves an existing link alone; and campaign-scoped listing returning only that campaign's characters, oldest first. 101 → 110 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Also re-ran a full manual smoke test against a real `next start` server covering the merged flow specifically: created an unlinked character via `/api/characters`, confirmed a non-owner's attempt to link it 403s, confirmed the owner linking their own character to their own campaign succeeds, confirmed a stranger creating a brand-new character pre-linked to a campaign they don't belong to is rejected, confirmed the public campaign-scoped endpoint and the campaign detail page both show the linked character, and confirmed the public player page shows the character with a working "Playing in <campaign>" link back.

Judgment call: chose not to try to reconcile the two commits' git history (e.g. rebasing, cherry-picking pieces of the discarded local commit) — the two implementations shared no code (different table shape, different files, different component names), so a clean rewrite on top of the already-pushed commit was more honest about what actually happened than a contrived merge commit would have been. The discarded local commit (`51a0e45`, campaign-scoped only, never pushed) was dropped entirely once its concept (optional campaign linking) was folded into the merged design.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c, still blocked) or the deployment decision (#3, still a human call).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #16, two-way post-session ratings)
Owner asked to keep going through the backlog for the rest of the day, one item at a time with a full test/commit/push cycle each. Picked #16 (ratings) as the next item: it's explicitly called out in this file as an owner-stated must-have and the trust layer the whole matching pitch depends on, and #3/#6c remain the only hard blockers (human decision / missing credentials) ahead of it in priority order.

Data model: a new `ratings` table (`id`, `campaign_id`, `rater_id`, `ratee_id`, `ratee_role` — `'dm'` or `'player'`, `stars` 1-5, `tags` a JSON-array string, timestamps), with a `UNIQUE (campaign_id, rater_id, ratee_id)` constraint so a second rating from the same rater upserts rather than duplicates — letting a rating be revised as a campaign goes on rather than locked in after one submission. `lib/ratings.ts`'s `rateCampaignParticipant()` derives each side's role in the campaign (`dm`, `player` via an approved-or-left membership, or `null`) and requires the rater and ratee to be on *opposite* roles — this both enforces the backlog's explicit DM<->player scope (no player-rates-player, no self-rating) and rejects strangers/pending-requesters/decliners in one check, without a separate permission function. `ratee_role` is stored directly on the row (denormalized from which side the ratee was playing) rather than derived via a join every read, since it's fixed at creation and this app already denormalizes similarly elsewhere.

Judgment call — scope narrowed from the backlog's literal "post-session" framing to per-campaign: this app has no discrete "session" entity (session log entries are free-text DM notes, not structured records with their own IDs a rating could attach to; `next_session_at` is a single upcoming date, not a history of past ones). Modeling a true per-session rating would need a new sessions table as a prerequisite, which felt like scope creep for this item — a per-campaign rating (upsertable, so it can reflect how things are going as the campaign progresses) is a reasonable, honest interpretation of "how has this DM/player been" and matches the "running average over many sessions" framing from the backlog's own carried-over design note better than a single one-off review would anyway.

Two curated tag sets (`DM_RATING_TAGS`, `PLAYER_RATING_TAGS` — 8 each, in `lib/types.ts` alongside `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`/`CHARACTER_AVATARS` as the established home for client-safe shared constants) since "great narrator" and "fair rulings" don't make sense for a player, and "team player"/"good roleplayer" don't make sense for a DM; a rating is validated against whichever set matches the *ratee's* role. `getUserRatingSummary(userId)` aggregates by `ratee_role` into `asDm`/`asPlayer` separately (a good DM and a good player are different signals, same reasoning as separating the two in the tag sets) — `average: null` when `count === 0` so a brand-new member renders as "Unrated," never as a 0-star score.

Routes: `GET`/`POST /api/campaigns/[id]/ratings` — GET returns the signed-in viewer's role, their eligible rating targets (the DM's approved/left players, or just the DM for a player) each with any existing rating, and the correct tag-option list for whichever role they're about to rate; POST creates/updates one rating. `components/RatingsPanel.tsx` (client, on the campaign detail page) renders nothing at all if the viewer has no relationship to the campaign — the panel's own emptiness is the "you're not eligible" signal, no separate error state needed in the UI. `/players/[id]` gained a "Reputation" block (As DM / As player, star display, rating count, top 3 tags) right under the display name, ahead of the bio — reputation is exactly the kind of at-a-glance trust signal a matchmaking app's whole pitch depends on, so it earns the prominent slot.

Notification: added a new `rating_prompt` type (flows through the existing `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`/`notify()`/preferences infrastructure automatically — no new plumbing needed there) fired from `leaveCampaign()` to both the leaving player ("rate the DM") and the DM ("rate them") — leaving is the one clear, already-instrumented "this relationship is wrapping up" moment in the app, versus e.g. prompting after every session log post (too noisy) or on a schedule (no session-completion event exists to hang it on).

Testing: `__tests__/unit/ratings.test.ts` (13 tests) covering both rating directions, rejecting player-to-player/self/stranger ratings, out-of-range stars, a tag from the wrong role's set, upsert-not-duplicate on a second rating, rating still being allowed after the ratee has left, the correct target list for a DM vs. a player, an empty list for someone with no relationship to the campaign, a fresh user reporting as unrated, multi-rating averaging with per-role tag tallies, and the `rating_prompt` notification firing to both sides on leave. 110 → 123 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server confirmed the full flow end-to-end: eligibility (approve → GET targets → rate), the invalid-tag-for-role rejection, the stranger rejection, the public reputation display rendering correctly, both `rating_prompt` notifications firing on leave, and the DM still being able to rate a player who's since left.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #17, session logbook kudos reactions)
Owner asked to keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day, one item per full implement/test/ship cycle. Picked #17 next: it's the next unblocked item after #16 in priority order (#3 and #6c remain the only hard blockers — human deployment decision and missing SMTP credentials respectively), and it builds directly on the session log that already exists.

Implementation: a new `session_log_kudos` table (`id`, `entry_id` FK, `user_id` FK, `created_at`) with a `UNIQUE (entry_id, user_id)` constraint — one kudos per user per entry, toggled rather than stackable, matching the backlog's own "lightweight tap-to-react" framing (a like button, not a counter a single person can inflate). `lib/sessionLog.ts` gained `listEntriesWithKudos(campaignId, viewerId)` (the existing `listEntries()` plus each entry's kudos count and whether the given viewer has already reacted) and `toggleKudos(entryId, userId)` (inserts if absent, deletes if present, gated by the same `hasPrivateAccess()` check the session log itself already uses — DM or an approved/left party member, not just the DM who can post). A fresh kudos (not an un-kudos, and never for reacting to your own entry) fires a new `session_log_kudos` notification to the entry's author, flowing through the existing `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`/`notify()`/preferences infrastructure with no additional plumbing. New route `POST /api/campaigns/[id]/log/[entryId]/kudos` calls `toggleKudos()`, mapping `SessionLogError` to 403 the same way every other session log route does. The existing `GET /api/campaigns/[id]/log` now calls `listEntriesWithKudos()` instead of `listEntries()`, and the campaign detail page passes the signed-in viewer's id down to `SessionLogPanel`, which renders a "👏 <count>" pill per entry — filled/highlighted when the viewer has already reacted, clickable by any signed-in party member (not gated to DM-only the way edit/delete are).

Judgment call: did not attempt to decide or build anything about broadening logbook visibility beyond the existing party (e.g. to a player's followers) — the backlog line explicitly flagged that as a separate product decision to make first, and this item's scope is the reaction affordance on entries that are already private to the party, not a visibility change.

Testing: extended `__tests__/unit/sessionLog.test.ts` with 4 new tests (up from 6 to 10) covering: an active party member giving then un-giving kudos on an entry (count and `viewerGaveKudos` correct at each step); a second viewer who hasn't reacted seeing `viewerGaveKudos: false` without affecting the count; the `session_log_kudos` notification firing to the author on a fresh kudos from someone else but not on self-kudos and not on un-giving; and a stranger with no relationship to the campaign being rejected. 123 → 127 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server (fresh SQLite db on a spare port) drove the full flow end-to-end via independent per-role cookie jars: signup DM + player, create campaign, join/approve, DM posts a session log entry, player gives kudos (count 1, `viewerGaveKudos` true for the player and false for the DM), player un-gives kudos (count back to 0), a signed-up stranger attempting kudos got a 403, and the DM giving kudos on their own entry did not add a duplicate notification beyond the one already fired by the player's earlier kudos.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #18, personal stats dashboard)
Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. Picked #18 next: the next unblocked item after #17 in priority order (#3 and #6c remain the only hard blockers), and its own backlog line already called out that it's derivable from existing data with no new data model.

Implementation: new `lib/stats.ts` with a single `getUserStats(userId)` returning `campaignsAsDm`, `campaignsAsPlayer`, `sessionsRun`, `sessionsPlayed`, `charactersCreated`, `systemsPlayed` (distinct), `mostPlayedSystem`, and `longestCampaign` (`{ id, title, sessionCount }` or null) — all computed via aggregation queries against `campaigns`, `memberships`, `session_log_entries`, and `characters`, no new tables. `UserStats` lives in `lib/types.ts` (the established client-safe home for shared shapes, per the `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`/`CHARACTER_AVATARS`/rating-tag precedent) since it needs to be importable by both a server component (`/players/[id]`) and a client component (`/profile`); `lib/stats.ts` re-exports the type for server-side callers. New presentational `components/StatsPanel.tsx` (no "use client" needed — pure display, no state or fetching of its own) renders identically from both call sites. `/players/[id]` calls `getUserStats()` directly (it's already a server component). `/profile` (a client component) gets stats from an extended `GET /api/profile` response (`stats: getUserStats(auth.user.id)` alongside the existing `profile`/`dming`/`playing`), one round trip, matching how that route already bundles multiple related pieces of data.

Judgment call — scope narrowed from the backlog line's literal wording: dropped "hours at the table" from what shipped. This app tracks no session start/end time or duration anywhere (session log entries are free-text recaps with just a `created_at`, not structured records with a duration field), so there's no honest way to derive actual hours played — estimating one (e.g. "assume every session is 3 hours") would be fabricating a number that reads as real data, which felt like the wrong tradeoff versus just not shipping that one stat yet. The other five ("sessions run"/"sessions played", "systems tried", "most played system", "longest campaign", "characters created") are all directly and honestly derivable from data this app already has. "Sessions run"/"sessions played" are explicitly documented in the code as a *proxy*, not a literal count: each session log entry represents one recap a DM posted after a session, so counting entries is the closest honest measure available — a DM who ran a session but forgot to post a recap won't show up, and this is called out in the doc comments rather than presented as ground truth. "Longest campaign" is measured by sessions logged, not wall-clock duration, for the same missing-duration-data reason.

Testing: new `__tests__/unit/stats.test.ts` (6 tests) covering: all-zero/empty/null output for a brand-new user; campaigns-DMed and sessionsRun counted correctly; campaigns-played and sessionsPlayed counted correctly, including entries posted *after* the player left (a left member's stats still reflect the sessions they were actually part of, matching the ratings feature's own "still allows rating after leaving" precedent); characters-created counted; distinct systems and the most-played system derived correctly across both a DM's and a player's combined DM+player campaigns; and the campaign with the most session log entries correctly picked as the longest campaign. 127 → 133 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server (fresh SQLite db, spare port): `GET /api/profile` for a brand-new user returned all-zero/empty/null stats; created a campaign, joined/approved a player, posted two session log entries, and re-fetched `/api/profile` for both the DM (`campaignsAsDm: 1`, `sessionsRun: 2`, `mostPlayedSystem: "5e"`, `longestCampaign` populated) and the player (`campaignsAsPlayer: 1`, `sessionsPlayed: 2`) — both correct; fetched the DM's public `/players/[id]` page HTML directly and confirmed the Stats section actually renders (grepped for "Sessions run", "Campaigns DMed", "Most played system", "Longest-running campaign", all present).

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. Picked #19 next: it's explicitly noted in its own backlog line as "the source chat's own strongest idea" and as feasible now that item 15 (character profiles) exists as a prerequisite — the next unblocked item after #18.

Data model: `characters` gained `status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'active' CHECK (status IN ('active','retired','fallen'))` and `epilogue TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT ''`; `campaigns` gained `danger_level TEXT CHECK (danger_level IS NULL OR danger_level IN ('low-lethality','moderate','high-lethality','deadly-osr'))`, nullable so "not set" is the honest default rather than forcing every DM to pick one. Added the same lightweight-migration pattern already established for `users.password_hash` (a `PRAGMA table_info` check plus `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN` for databases created before this change) — SQLite can't add a CHECK constraint via `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`, so migrated databases rely on `lib/characters.ts`/`lib/campaigns.ts` validation rather than the schema itself for those two columns; new databases get the full constraint from the `CREATE TABLE` statements.

`CHARACTER_STATUSES`/`CHARACTER_STATUS_LABELS` and `DANGER_LEVELS`/`DANGER_LEVEL_LABELS` live in `lib/types.ts` alongside the established client-safe constants (`NOTIFICATION_TYPES`, `CHARACTER_AVATARS`, the rating tag sets). `lib/characters.ts`'s `createCharacter`/`updateCharacter` accept optional `status`/`epilogue` (defaulting a new character to `"active"` with an empty epilogue; an unrecognized status value is ignored and falls back to the current/default rather than erroring, matching the existing `avatarEmoji` fallback pattern). New `getCampaignChronicle(campaignId)` aggregates currently-linked characters by status into `{ active, retired, fallen, total }`. `lib/campaigns.ts`'s `updateCampaign` gained a `dangerLevel` update (undefined = unchanged, null = clear, a recognized level = set), validated against `DANGER_LEVELS`.

UI: `CharacterManager`'s create/edit form gained a status `<select>` and, only shown once status isn't "active," an epilogue textarea. `CharacterSummary` (shared by all three character-display surfaces) shows a status badge next to the name when not active (styled distinctly for "fallen" — a red-tinted border — versus "retired") and the epilogue in italics beneath the backstory. `DmControls`'s edit form gained a danger-level `<select>` with an explicit "not a scoreboard" caption. The campaign detail page shows the danger level as a small badge in the header (visible to anyone, not gated behind private access — the whole point is a heads-up for a prospective player deciding whether to join) and a one-line "Chronicle: N characters passed through · X still adventuring · Y retired · Z fallen" summary above the existing "Characters at this table" list (only the non-zero segments are shown).

Judgment call: scoped "characters passed through" in the chronicle to characters *currently* linked to the campaign, not a true historical roster of every character that was ever part of it. This app's character-campaign link (from item 15) is a single current pointer (`campaign_id`, nullable, one at a time) with no retained history of past links — once a character is unlinked or relinked elsewhere, this app has no record it was ever there. Building a full "every character who ever passed through, even after leaving" history would need a separate link-history table as its own follow-up; reading the current chronicle as "right now," not "ever," is an honest reflection of what this app's existing data model actually tracks, matching the same kind of proxy-not-literal framing already used for backlog #18's "sessions run/played."

Testing: extended `__tests__/unit/characters.test.ts` with 7 new tests (19 → 26) covering: a new character defaulting to active status with an empty epilogue; creating directly as retired/fallen with an epilogue; updating status and epilogue independently of other fields; rejecting an over-length epilogue; an unrecognized status value falling back to the default; chronicle counts aggregating correctly by status among currently-linked characters (and excluding an unlinked one); and a character dropping out of the chronicle once unlinked. Extended `__tests__/unit/campaigns.test.ts` with 3 new tests (10 → 13) covering: a new campaign defaulting to a null danger level; the DM setting, changing, and clearing it; and an omitted `dangerLevel` on update leaving an existing value untouched. 133 → 143 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server (fresh SQLite db, spare port) drove the full flow end-to-end: DM set a danger level (visible in the campaign header HTML), a non-DM's attempt to change it correctly errored, a player joined/was approved/created a character linked to the campaign (active by default), the campaign page's Chronicle line correctly read "1 character passed through · 1 still adventuring," the player then marked their character as fallen with an epilogue, the Chronicle line updated to reflect the fallen character, the public `/players/[id]` page rendered both the "Fallen" badge and the epilogue text, and both an invalid danger level and an invalid character status were correctly rejected (400) by their respective API routes.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #22, coarse location)
Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. Picked #22 next: items #20 (substitute player workflow) and #23/#24 (freemium/payments, native iOS) are explicitly flagged as either a much heavier multi-night effort or blocked on business/human decisions this session can't make unattended; #21 (swipe/deck discovery UI) is a lower-priority UX layer over data this app can already list; #22's own backlog line explicitly calls out the coarse (non-geocoded) version as "feasible now," making it the next reasonably-scoped item.

Data model: `profiles` and `campaigns` each gained a `location TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` column — free text, deliberately not a geocoded address or lat/long pair, matching the backlog's own explicit scope cut (precise distance search needs a maps API key this session doesn't have, plus a privacy-surface decision about storing exact locations, both flagged as blocking a *precise* version, not the coarse one shipped here). Added the same lightweight-migration pattern used for every prior schema change this session (PRAGMA check + `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` for pre-existing databases).

`lib/profiles.ts`'s `upsertProfile()` accepts an optional `location`, trimmed and capped at 200 characters like the other profile text fields. `lib/campaigns.ts`'s `createCampaign()` accepts an optional `location` (defaulting to `""`), `updateCampaign()` accepts a `location` update, and `listCampaigns()` gained a `location` filter option — a case-insensitive substring match (`LOWER(location) LIKE ... ESCAPE '\'`) reusing the exact same `escapeLikePattern()` helper and `%`/`_`-as-literal-character convention already established for the free-text keyword search (backlog #7), so filtering by "Austin" matches a campaign whose location is "Austin, TX" without also treating a literal `%` or `_` in someone's location string as a wildcard.

UI: a "Location" text input added to `/profile`'s self-edit form, the "Post a campaign" form, and `DmControls`' campaign-edit form, all following the exact same pattern as this app's other free-text fields (availability, preferred systems, description). Displayed on `/players/[id]` (a new "Location" section, shown only if set), the campaign detail page header (`&middot; <location>` alongside system/DM/headcount/danger-level), and each campaign card on the `/campaigns` browse list. The browse page's filter form gained a "Location" input alongside the existing Search/System/Sort controls, threaded through pagination links the same way `system`/`q` already are.

Testing: extended `__tests__/unit/profiles.test.ts` with 2 new assertions/tests (default empty location, saving/re-reading it, and rejecting an over-length value) and `__tests__/unit/campaigns.test.ts` with 2 new tests (a campaign defaulting to an empty location and having it set via create/update, and browse-page-style filtering by a case-insensitive location substring including a no-match case). 143 → 146 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server: a fresh profile's location defaulted to `""`, saving and re-reading `"Austin, TX"` round-tripped correctly and rendered on the public `/players/[id]` page, two campaigns created with different locations were correctly filtered by `?location=austin` and `?location=online` via the API, both locations rendered correctly on the browse page HTML, and an over-200-character location on campaign creation was correctly rejected with a 400.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3). Precise geolocation/distance search remains explicitly out of scope, per the backlog line's own stated blocker (no maps API key, and a privacy decision not yet made).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #21, swipe/deck discovery UI)
Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. Picked #21 next: items #20 (substitute player workflow) and #23/#24 (freemium/payments, native iOS) remain explicitly flagged as either a much heavier multi-night effort or blocked on business/human decisions this session can't make unattended; #21, while ranked lower priority than items 15-20 by the backlog's own ordering, is explicitly called out as feasible now (a UX layer over data this app can already list and filter), making it the next reasonably one-night-scoped item once #22 was done.

Implementation: new `components/DiscoverDeck.tsx` (client component) takes the same filtered campaign list the existing browse view already computes and presents it one card at a time — title, system, DM name, headcount/capacity, location, danger level, and description — with a "Pass" button (advances a local `useState` index, no persistence) and a "View & join" link straight to that campaign's own detail page. `/campaigns` gained a List/Discover toggle (a `view` query param, so it's linkable/shareable like the existing filters) right next to "Post a campaign." In discover mode the page fetches a larger batch (50) instead of the list view's page size of 10, and skips rendering numbered pagination — a "swipe through these" flow doesn't fit paging, one continuous deck does.

Two judgment calls worth flagging. First: no join/leave action lives inside the deck itself — clicking "View & join" always lands on the real campaign detail page, which is already the single source of truth for a viewer's membership state (pending/approved/etc.) via `JoinLeaveControls`. Duplicating that logic into the deck card would mean two parallel places membership state could drift, for a feature explicitly scoped as "an alternate presentation," not new capability — so the deck only decides *what to show*, never *what to do about it*. Second: scoped to campaign cards only, not also player/DM cards as swipeable entities (the backlog line mentions "player, DM, and campaign cards") — this app has no direct messaging or player-to-player matching for a "connect" action on a person-card to plug into yet, so building swipeable people-cards now would mean inventing a whole connect/match workflow this backlog item doesn't define, well beyond "make browsing more visual." Campaigns are the one entity this app's browse/filter/join loop already fully supports end to end, so that's what the deck wraps.

Testing: no new unit tests — this item added no `lib/` logic at all, only a presentational client component driven by data (`listCampaigns`, `approvedHeadcount`, `getUserById`) that already has full unit-test coverage from prior backlog items. This matches the existing convention in this codebase of not writing component-level rendering tests for presentational UI (`CharacterManager`, `RatingsPanel`, `SessionLogPanel`, etc. have none either) — there's no jsdom/Testing Library setup in this project, only `lib/` unit tests and real-server HTTP integration tests. 146 tests still green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server: created two campaigns sharing a unique test system, confirmed the default list view still renders both, confirmed `?view=discover` returns 200 and the server-rendered payload contains the first card's full data (title, description, system, location, headcount, DM name) plus the "1 of 2" progress indicator and both the "Pass"/"View & join" controls and the List/Discover toggle links.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #26, character portrait image upload)
Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. Revisited item #26 (owner-requested, added earlier this session) rather than moving on to a genuinely blocked item: its own backlog text had already documented three storage options and flagged only the storage decision as the blocker, one of which (base64-in-SQLite) needs no new infrastructure and no credentials at all — making this a reasonable "feasible now" candidate on closer look, not something that actually has to wait for item #3.

Data model: `characters` gained a nullable `portrait_data_url TEXT` column (the standard lightweight-migration pattern for existing databases, same as every prior schema change tonight). `null` means no uploaded portrait, in which case `avatar_emoji` is shown as before — fully backward compatible, no existing character's display changes.

Validation, in `lib/characters.ts`'s new `validatePortraitDataUrl()`: rejects anything not matching `^data:image\/(png|jpeg|jpg|webp|gif);base64,` (wrong MIME type or not a data URL at all), and rejects anything over 280,000 characters — base64 inflates size by roughly a third, so that string-length cap corresponds to source images up to roughly 200KB, small enough to not meaningfully bloat the SQLite file while still allowing a real compressed photo or drawing. `createCharacter`/`updateCharacter` both thread an optional `portraitDataUrl` through this validator (undefined = unchanged on update, null = explicitly remove, a valid data URL = set/replace). The zod schemas on both character API routes mirror the same length cap as an early reject before the request body is even fully parsed, with the real validation staying in `lib/characters.ts` as the source of truth.

UI: `CharacterManager`'s form gained a file `<input accept="image/png,image/jpeg,image/webp,image/gif">` next to the existing emoji picker, with a live preview (the uploaded image if present, the emoji otherwise) and a "Remove" button once a portrait is set. Client-side, `FileReader.readAsDataURL()` converts the selected file to a data URL, after a size check (200KB raw, mirroring the server's ~200KB-source budget) that rejects an oversized file immediately rather than after a wasted upload+encode round trip. `CharacterSummary` (shared by every character-display surface — `/profile`, `/players/[id]`, and a campaign's page) renders the portrait as a small rounded `<img>` when set, falling back to the emoji span otherwise, so all three views stay in sync automatically.

Judgment call, explicitly flagged: this bypasses the backlog line's own note that the item "should land after item #3 (deployment decision)." That guidance made sense for the local-disk and object-storage options (both genuinely need to know where the app runs, or need their own credentials), but doesn't apply to base64-in-SQLite — a small, size-capped text column works identically no matter where this app eventually deploys. Revisiting the note on its merits rather than treating it as an unconditional gate felt like the honest reading here, especially since the owner explicitly asked for this feature. Also explicitly not built: server-side resizing/compression. An oversized or wrong-format upload is rejected with a clear message, not transformed — keeping this pass to validation only, matching the backlog's own "basic upload hygiene" framing rather than a full image-processing pipeline.

Testing: extended `__tests__/unit/characters.test.ts` with 6 new tests (26 → 32): a new character defaulting to a null portrait; creating directly with a valid portrait data URL; rejecting a non-image-MIME or malformed (non-data-URL) string; rejecting an over-length data URL; the owner setting, replacing, and removing a portrait via three successive updates; and an existing portrait staying untouched when omitted from an unrelated update. 146 → 152 tests, all green; build and lint both clean (including fixing a real ESLint gotcha caught during this item: a two-line `// eslint-disable-next-line` comment only suppresses the literal next line — the second comment line consumed that slot, leaving the actual `<img>` element on the third line unprotected and still flagged; fixed by collapsing the disable comment plus its justification onto a single line immediately before the element, in both `CharacterManager.tsx` and `CharacterSummary.tsx`). Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server: a fresh character defaulted to a null portrait; setting a small valid `data:image/png;base64,...` value via `PATCH` persisted correctly; the public `/players/[id]` page's rendered HTML contained the actual `data:image/png;base64,...` string as an `<img src>` attribute (confirmed by direct grep of the response body, not just the API response); an oversized (300,000+ character) data URL was rejected with 400; a non-image MIME type and a garbage non-data-URL string were both rejected with 403 (`CharacterError` mapped to 403, consistent with every other character-update rejection); and removing the portrait (`portraitDataUrl: null`) correctly cleared it back to null.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3) — this item's whole point was proving it didn't actually need to wait on #3.

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #27 phase 1, structured weekly availability grid)
Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. All six of the reasonably one-night-scoped, currently-unblocked backlog items had been shipped (#17, #18, #19, #22, #21, #26); the remaining items are each either a genuine human/business decision this session can't make (#3 deployment, #23 payments, #24 App Store distribution, all blocked on credentials or owner decisions), blocked on missing credentials (#6c email), open-ended process notes rather than scoped features (#9-#11), or explicitly flagged in the backlog's own text as needing to be scoped as their own multi-night effort rather than attempted in one pass (#20 substitute-player workflow, #27 calendar availability). Rather than force a full multi-night item into one pass, or stop while a defensible slice remained, took #27's own suggested scoping literally: it names "(a) a new data model for recurring weekly time-slots per user... (b) a calendar/grid picker UI component... then (c) timezone handling... and (d) the actual search/filter" as the shape of the work, and explicitly says to "scope as its own multi-part effort (data model + picker UI first, then the search/matching layer)." This session did exactly that first slice — (a) and (b) only — and stopped there.

Data model: new `availability_slots` table (`user_id`, `day_of_week` 0-6, `block` one of morning/afternoon/evening/night, composite primary key on all three) — a plain new table, not a migration, since it's additive with no prior column to backfill. `day_of_week` is explicitly documented as an app-level display-order index (0=Monday..6=Sunday) unrelated to `Date.prototype.getDay()` (which is 0=Sunday) — there's deliberately no calendar date backing a *recurring* weekly pattern, so tying it to JS's day numbering would just invite confusion later. No timezone is stored; the grid represents the user's own subjective week, same scope-limiting choice as the existing free-text `availability` field.

Backend: `lib/availability.ts` exports `getAvailabilitySlots(userId)` and `setAvailabilitySlots(userId, slots)` — the latter is a full-replace (delete-then-reinsert in one transaction), not a per-cell toggle endpoint, because the UI always has and submits the complete current grid state rather than tracking individual diffs. Validates each slot's day (integer 0-6) and block (one of the four known values) before writing anything, and silently de-duplicates repeated day/block pairs rather than erroring on them (a user's own double-click shouldn't be a 400). `AVAILABILITY_DAYS`/`AVAILABILITY_BLOCKS`/`AvailabilityBlock`/`AvailabilitySlot` all live in `lib/types.ts` (not `lib/availability.ts`), following this session's established client-safe-constants convention so the new `AvailabilityGrid` client component can import them without pulling in `lib/db.ts`.

API: folded into the existing `GET`/`PUT /api/profile` route rather than a new endpoint — `GET` now also returns `availabilitySlots`, and `PUT`'s body schema gained an optional `availabilitySlots` array (max 28 entries, one per possible cell) that's a full-replace when present and leaves the stored grid untouched when omitted, matching every other field's partial-update semantics on this same route.

UI: new `components/AvailabilityGrid.tsx`, a controlled 7×4 table of toggle buttons (`aria-pressed`, `aria-label`s naming the day and block for screen readers) that takes `slots`/`onChange`/`editable` props and owns no fetch/save logic itself, matching `CharacterManager`'s controlled-form pattern. Used in two places: editable on `/profile` (folded into the existing single profile form and its one Save button, submitted alongside bio/systems/availability/location in the same `PUT`), and read-only on `/players/[id]` (only rendered when the user has at least one slot set, so a user who hasn't touched this feature yet shows nothing new).

Judgment call, explicitly flagged: kept the existing free-text `availability` field on profiles as-is, alongside the new structured grid, rather than replacing it as the backlog line's longer-term framing implies ("replace the free-text `availability` field... with structured, queryable availability"). Replacing it now would risk silently discarding whatever users have already written there, for no present benefit — the matching/search layer that would actually need the structured data instead of the free text doesn't exist yet in this phase. Revisit the free-text field's fate once phase 2 (search/matching) is actually built. Also explicitly not built in this phase: timezone handling (item (c)) and the search/filter query itself (item (d)) — both remain for a future phase, as the backlog's own scoping note anticipated.

Testing: new `__tests__/unit/availability.test.ts` (8 tests) covering: an empty grid for a brand-new user; saving a set of slots and reading them back in day-then-block order; a second save fully replacing rather than merging the grid; clearing the grid with an empty array; de-duplicating repeated day/block pairs instead of erroring; rejecting an out-of-range day; rejecting an unrecognized block; and per-user grid isolation. 152 → 160 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server (fresh SQLite db, spare port): a fresh profile's `GET` returned an empty `availabilitySlots` array; `PUT` with two slots persisted and round-tripped correctly on the next `GET`; `PUT` with an invalid block ("midnight") and an out-of-range day (9) were both correctly rejected with 400 and a clear zod error message; a `PUT` touching only an unrelated field (`bio`) left the previously-saved grid untouched; the public `/players/[id]` page's rendered HTML contained exactly 28 `aria-pressed` grid cells (7 days × 4 blocks) reflecting the saved slots; and a brand-new user's `/players/[id]` page correctly rendered no "Weekly availability" section at all (grid-empty case hides the block entirely, matching the existing convention for other optional profile sections like location/availability text).

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #20 phase 1, sub request + volunteer pool)
Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. With #27's phase 1 shipped, the only other item with a backlog-endorsed narrow first slice left was #20 (substitute player workflow) — its own text names the same three-phase shape used for #27: "first the request + volunteer pool, then the three-way approval state machine, then guardrail enforcement." This session shipped exactly that first phase and stopped there, the same discipline applied to #27 earlier tonight.

Data model: two new tables, `sub_requests` (campaign_id, requester_id, note, status open/filled/cancelled) and `sub_volunteers` (request_id, volunteer_id, message, unique per request+volunteer so volunteering twice updates rather than duplicates) — both purely additive, no migration needed.

Backend (`lib/subRequests.ts`): `createSubRequest` is gated behind `hasPrivateAccess` (the same DM-or-approved-member boundary as session log/party notes) since only someone actually at the table has a session to fill. `listSubRequestsForCampaign` and `listOpenSubRequests` are deliberately public/ungated — like the roster and character list already on a campaign's page, inviting an outside volunteer is the whole point, so browsing isn't restricted to existing members. Both return a `SubRequestSummary` (campaign title/system, requester name, volunteer count, whether the current viewer has already volunteered) computed via a shared `withContext` join helper, with `SubRequestSummary`/`SubVolunteerWithName` living in `lib/types.ts` rather than `lib/subRequests.ts` — same client-safe-types convention established earlier this session for `UserStats`/`AvailabilitySlot`, since these shapes are consumed by client components. `volunteerForSubRequest` rejects the requester volunteering for their own request and rejects volunteering on a non-open request, upserts (not duplicates) on a repeat call from the same volunteer, and only notifies (a new `sub_volunteer` notification type, added to `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`/`NOTIFICATION_LABELS` so it flows through the existing preferences UI automatically) the requester and DM on a genuinely fresh volunteer, not on a message edit. `listVolunteers` restricts volunteer identities to the requester or DM — unlike the request itself, volunteer identities aren't broadcast to everyone browsing the pool. `setSubRequestStatus` only allows a single open -> filled/cancelled transition (no reopening), restricted to the requester or DM.

API: `GET`/`POST /api/campaigns/[id]/subs` (list is public, post requires sign-in + the access check above), `GET /api/subs` (the app-wide browsable pool, public, same reasoning as the existing public `GET /api/campaigns`), `POST`/`DELETE /api/subs/[id]/volunteer`, `GET /api/subs/[id]/volunteers`, `POST /api/subs/[id]/status`.

UI: `components/SubRequestPanel.tsx` (client, self-fetching like `RatingsPanel`) sits on the campaign detail page — a post form for the DM/approved members, a list of that campaign's own requests (open and past), and for the requester/DM a "view volunteers" toggle plus "mark filled"/"cancel" buttons. A new `/subs` page (server component, matching `/campaigns`'s pattern of server-rendering the list and delegating just the interactive sliver to a small client component) is the app-wide browsable pool, with a new `components/SubVolunteerAction.tsx` handling the volunteer/withdraw control per row. Added a "Find a sub" link to the NavBar.

Judgment call, explicitly flagged: "mark filled" is just a status flag the requester/DM sets after reading the volunteer list and deciding off-system who's actually subbing in — there's no seat assignment, no character handoff, and no guardrail enforcement in this phase. That's phase 2/3 territory per the backlog's own scoping, deferred exactly as planned.

Testing: new `__tests__/unit/subRequests.test.ts` (14 tests) covering: posting defaulting to open (DM and player); rejecting a post from someone with no relationship to the campaign; rejecting an over-length note; open requests appearing in the app-wide pool and disappearing once filled while still visible on the campaign's own list; listings enriched with campaign/requester context and volunteer counts; a stranger volunteering with `viewerHasVolunteered` correctly reflecting per-viewer state; rejecting the requester volunteering for their own request; rejecting volunteering on a non-open request; a second volunteer call updating the message instead of duplicating; withdrawing a volunteer; restricting the volunteer list to the requester/DM; the `sub_volunteer` notification firing once each to the requester and DM on a fresh volunteer but not on a message update; the requester/DM marking a request filled only from open (and a second status change on an already-terminal request being rejected); and rejecting a status change from someone who isn't the requester or DM. 160 -> 174 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server (fresh SQLite db, spare port) drove the full flow end-to-end via independent per-role cookie jars: a stranger's attempt to post a sub request 403'd; the approved player posted one; the app-wide pool showed it (including while signed out); an unrelated signed-up user volunteered with a message; the requester attempting to volunteer for their own request 403'd; a stranger attempting to view the volunteer list 403'd; the requester successfully viewed it; the DM marked the request filled; a second status-change attempt on the now-terminal request 403'd; the app-wide pool no longer showed it while the campaign's own list still did (correctly showing "filled"); and restarting the server against the same SQLite file confirmed the data persisted correctly.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. With both #20 and #27 now at their backlog-endorsed "phase 1" stopping points, going further into either one's phase 2 (a three-way approval/character-custody state machine for #20, or timezone handling and a real search/matching query for #27) would mean attempting a piece explicitly flagged as needing its own dedicated scoping effort, not a natural continuation of tonight's pattern of small, complete slices. No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

2026-09-06 (live session, continued: backlog #20 phase 2, owner/DM approval + temporary custody)
Continuing the owner's "keep iterating through the backlog for the rest of the day" instruction. The owner explicitly asked to scope backlog #20's phase 2 next (rather than pick a different item), so this entry documents both the design decisions made before writing any code and the implementation itself.

Before implementing, three structural questions were put to the owner rather than guessed at, since this is explicitly "the heaviest single item" in the backlog and getting the shape wrong would mean throwaway work: (1) how strict should "the table" be as the third approval vote — the answer was owner + DM required, the rest of the active party notified but not a blocking gate, which turns "three-way approval" into a bounded two-approver state machine; (2) should the workflow require a request to name a specific character — yes, since there needs to be an "owner" and something to hand temporary custody of; (3) build the full (two-approver) workflow in one pass rather than slicing further — yes. These three answers shaped everything below.

Data model: `sub_requests` gained a nullable `character_id` (set at creation, validated to be a character the requester owns AND currently linked to this campaign — this is what lets "the requester" and "the character owner" be treated as the same person everywhere downstream, with no separate owner concept needed). New `sub_placements` table (request_id, volunteer_id, guardrails_note, owner_approved, dm_approved, status: pending/confirmed/declined/cancelled). `characters` gained a nullable `temp_pilot_user_id` — a pure display marker ("currently piloted by X"), set when a placement is confirmed, cleared by an explicit "end sub" action (this app has no session-duration tracking to clear it automatically).

Backend (`lib/subPlacements.ts`): `createPlacement` is restricted to the requester or DM, only for a character-linked open request, only for someone who's actually in that request's volunteer pool, and only one pending placement per request at a time. `ownerReview`/`dmReview` each independently approve or decline; either one declining ends the placement immediately without waiting on the other (no reason to make someone wait on a review that's already moot). Once both approve (in either order — `resolveIfBothApproved` runs after each review and checks both flags, not just the one that just changed), the placement is confirmed, `characters.temp_pilot_user_id` is set, and the parent `sub_requests.status` becomes `filled`. `cancelPlacement` lets the requester/DM back out of a still-pending placement outright (e.g. the volunteer changed their mind). `endSub` clears the custody marker and is restricted to the character's owner or the campaign's DM; the confirmed placement record itself is left alone as history, only the character's live state changes. A new `sub_placement_pending`/`sub_placement_resolved` notification pair (added to `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`, flowing through the existing preferences UI automatically) notifies whichever of {owner, DM} didn't act plus the volunteer on creation, and notifies the volunteer + owner + DM + every other currently-active party member on any resolution (confirmed/declined/cancelled) — this is exactly what "the table is informational" means in practice: everyone finds out, but nobody's presence is a gate.

API: `POST /api/campaigns/[id]/subs` now accepts an optional `characterId`. New routes: `GET`/`POST /api/subs/[id]/placements` (list is public/informational like the request itself; create is access-checked), `POST /api/subs/placements/[placementId]/owner-review`, `POST /api/subs/placements/[placementId]/dm-review`, `POST /api/subs/placements/[placementId]/cancel`, `POST /api/characters/[id]/end-sub`.

UI: `SubRequestPanel` gained a character picker on the post form (only shown if the poster has characters linked to this campaign), a "Select for approval" button next to each volunteer in the owner/DM's volunteer list, and a placement card showing both approval states, an editable guardrails textarea for the owner, approve/decline buttons for whichever reviewer is viewing, and a cancel option. `CharacterSummary` gained a `pilotName`/`canEndSub` prop pair rendering the "Currently piloted by" badge; ending the sub is a dedicated new `components/EndSubButton.tsx` client component rather than a callback prop, because `CharacterSummary` is rendered directly by Server Components (the campaign page, the public player page) that can't pass event-handler functions across the server/client boundary — only a genuine Client Component can own that click handler. `/api/characters` was extended to resolve and include `pilotName` alongside the existing `campaignTitle` enrichment, following that route's own established pattern.

Judgment call, explicitly flagged: guardrails are a free-text note the owner attaches, not the structured "blocks permanent/irreversible actions by default unless explicitly allowed" checkbox system the backlog line originally sketched, and there is no programmatic enforcement of them at all — this app is a matchmaking/session-tracking tool with no in-game action to gate, so "enforcement" can only ever mean "the DM and volunteer can read what the owner asked for." Building a structured checkbox system for guardrails that are inherently unenforceable felt like complexity without a real payoff; a note serves the actual need (communicating the owner's wishes) just as well.

Testing: new `__tests__/unit/subPlacements.test.ts` (18 tests) covering the full state machine — placement creation by requester/DM, rejecting creation by a stranger/for a non-volunteer/for a characterless request, rejecting a second pending placement, confirming with custody transfer regardless of approval order, either reviewer's decline ending things immediately, rejecting a review from the wrong person or on an already-resolved placement, cancel-and-reopen, end-sub access control and its effect on the character record vs. the historical placement record, the resolved-notification fan-out to all four roles, and listing placements including resolved ones. Extended `__tests__/unit/subRequests.test.ts` with 4 tests for the new `characterId` parameter (accepting it, rejecting a character the requester doesn't own, rejecting one not linked to this campaign, defaulting to null). 174 → 196 tests, all green; build and lint both clean. Manual smoke test against a real `next start` server drove the complete flow end-to-end across four independent users (DM, requester/owner, volunteer, stranger): character-linked request creation, volunteering, a stranger blocked from creating a placement, a duplicate-placement attempt blocked, the DM blocked from giving the owner's review and vice versa, owner approval with a guardrails note, confirming that custody stayed unset with only one approval in, DM approval completing the confirmation (custody set, request marked filled), the "Currently piloted by" badge rendering correctly on both the public player page and the campaign page (including the DM's "End sub" control), and ending the sub correctly clearing the badge.

No changes to email/SMTP (#6c) or the deployment decision (#3).

The rating only appears on a campaign's own detail page for now; browse-card surfacing is a natural, easy follow-up.

Manual smoke test against two real `next start` servers (fresh SQLite db, spare ports) confirmed end-to-end over HTTP: an anonymous `GET` on the new endpoint succeeds and returns the public summary; the DM's own `POST` 403s; a pending (not-yet-approved) player's rating attempt implicitly rejected via `canRate: false`; after PATCH-approving the player's membership, their `POST` succeeds and the aggregate updates correctly (average, count, tag tallies); and an invalid tag is rejected with a clear 403 message.

2026-09-07 (live session, backlog #31: direct messaging, 1:1)
Continuing the same-day chain of one-item-at-a-time increments. Picked backlog item #31, the next unblocked item in priority order: #3 (deployment decision) and #6c (email/SMTP) remain blocked on human decisions/missing credentials as documented throughout this file, and #31 was explicitly flagged as "probably the single highest-leverage item in this whole batch" when the twelve-item batch (#31-42) was added earlier the same day.

Design questions resolved before coding, per this item's own backlog text:
* **Who can message whom.** Chose "any signed-in user can message any other signed-in user," no prerequisite relationship. This app already has several ungated, open-to-anyone surfaces — browsing/joining campaigns has no prior relationship requirement, the sub-request volunteer pool (backlog #20) is browsable and volunteer-able by any signed-in stranger, and public player profiles (`/players/[id]`) are visible to everyone. Gating messaging behind "you share a campaign" or "you have a pending join request" would have been a new, unexplained exception to that pattern, and would also work against the actual use case the backlog line itself named first: "a prospective player messaging a DM *before* requesting to join" — which by definition happens before any relationship exists. The one hard rule is you can't message yourself.
* **Data model.** A `messages` table (`id`, `sender_id`, `recipient_id`, `body`, `read`, `created_at`) — no separate `conversations` table. A "conversation" is simply the unique unordered pair of `(sender_id, recipient_id)` values across a user's messages, computed at query time rather than tracked as its own row, since nothing about a conversation needs its own identity or mutable state beyond the messages inside it (no title, no archival, no group membership — those would justify a real entity; a 1:1 thread doesn't). This also sidesteps a canonicalization question a `conversations` table would need (e.g. always storing the lower user id first) — every query just checks both directions.
* **Unread tracking "wired into the existing notification system."** Read literally: `sendMessage()` calls the existing `notify()` with a new `message_received` type, going through the same `NOTIFICATION_TYPES`/`NOTIFICATION_LABELS`/preferences/mute machinery as every other notification type, and the NavBar's existing unread badge (SSE + polling fallback, backlog #6e) picks it up automatically with zero NavBar changes needed. Deliberately did *not* build a second, parallel "unread messages" live-badge system on top of the `messages.read` column — that column exists for the inbox/conversation UI's own bold/unread styling (e.g. showing an unread indicator on a specific conversation row), a separate, more local concern from the app-wide notification badge, and building two overlapping live-count systems for the same underlying event felt like exactly the kind of extra the task's own scope guidance (no read receipts, no typing indicators) was steering away from.

Implementation: `lib/messages.ts` (new) — `MessageError`, `sendMessage(senderId, recipientId, body)` (validates not-self, recipient exists via `getUserById`, non-blank, ≤4000 chars, trims; fires the `message_received` notification to the recipient), `listConversations(userId)` (queries every message involving the user newest-first, then does a single JS pass keeping the first — i.e. most recent — row per conversation partner; this both dedupes into one row per conversation and gets the most-recently-active-first ordering in the same pass, deliberately chosen over a SQL window function to stay consistent with this codebase's existing style of a plain query plus a JS enrichment/grouping pass, e.g. `lib/subRequests.ts`'s `withContext`), `getConversation(userId, otherUserId)` (the full thread, oldest first — no separate access check needed since the API route only ever calls it with the signed-in user as one of the two ids, unlike `hasPrivateAccess` where a third party's access needs checking), and `markConversationRead(userId, otherUserId)` (flips `read` on the incoming side of the thread only).

Schema: `messages` table (see `lib/db.ts`) plus a new nullable `related_user_id` column on the existing `notifications` table (with a lightweight `ALTER TABLE` migration for pre-existing databases, matching every other schema-change convention already established in this file's "Tooling notes"). This was the one deviation from a pure additive change: every notification before this pointed at a campaign (`campaign_id`) or nowhere; a `message_received` notification needs to link to a *conversation* instead, so `related_user_id` is a second, optional link target alongside `campaign_id` rather than repurposing that column or inventing a generic polymorphic link column. `notify()`'s signature grew a fifth, optional `relatedUserId: string | null = null` parameter — every one of the dozen-plus existing call sites (`lib/memberships.ts`, `lib/sessionLog.ts`, `lib/campaigns.ts`, `lib/subRequests.ts`, `lib/subPlacements.ts`, `lib/schedule.ts`, `lib/partyNotes.ts`) still passes exactly four positional args and is completely unaffected.

Routes: `GET /api/messages` (the inbox — `listConversations` for the signed-in user) and `GET`/`POST /api/messages/[userId]` (the thread with one specific other user — GET returns the messages plus `viewerId` and the other user's display name, and marks that direction read as a side effect of opening it; POST sends a new message *to* that user, mirroring the campaign-scoped nested-resource route shape already used by e.g. `/api/campaigns/[id]/subs`). Both go through `requireUser()`/`errorResponse()` exactly like every other route in this app. Note the two different failure modes for an invalid recipient: GET checks existence explicitly and 404s ("User not found"), while POST's `sendMessage()` throws a `MessageError` that the route maps to 400 ("Recipient not found") — a deliberate small asymmetry, not an oversight: GET is about *finding a page/thread* (404 is the right status for "there's no such resource to view"), POST is about *validating a request body* (400 is the right status for "the recipientId you sent is invalid"), and this matches how this app already treats not-found vs. bad-input elsewhere (e.g. a campaign 404s on its own detail page but a bad campaign id in a join-request body 400s).

UI: `app/messages/page.tsx` (client, self-fetching inbox list, redirect-to-`/signin` on 401 — same pattern as `ProfilePage`/`NotificationsPage`) and `app/messages/[userId]/page.tsx` (a thin async server component that awaits the dynamic route param and hands a plain `otherUserId` string prop to `components/ConversationView.tsx`, a client component doing the actual fetch/send/redirect work — this two-layer split matches the campaign detail page's own convention of a server component awaiting `params` and passing plain props down to client subcomponents, rather than having the client page component itself unwrap a `Promise` param via React's `use()`, which would have been a new, unprecedented pattern in this codebase). `ConversationView` renders messages as left/right-aligned chat bubbles (right-aligned + shaded for the viewer's own messages, determined by comparing `sender_id` to the `viewerId` the GET response returns) with a textarea composer below. `components/NavBar.tsx` gained a plain "Messages" link (no separate badge — see the unread-tracking judgment call above). `app/players/[id]/page.tsx` gained a "Message" button next to the display name, shown only when a viewer is signed in and isn't viewing their own profile — the natural place to start a new conversation, matching how every other cross-user action in this app (rate, follow a DM/roster name to `/players/[id]`, etc.) is anchored to that same public profile page.
